Lemma 2.12

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm). Then, for every h>0h>0 one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(C_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(0,\infty ))\subset \mathcal {A}_{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1},Hh}(\mathbb {H})$$\end{document}L(CM,h(0,∞))⊂AM+1,Hh(H), where H>1H>1 is the constant appearing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}:C_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(0,\infty )\rightarrow \mathcal {A}_{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1},Hh}(\mathbb {H})$$\end{document}L:CM,h(0,∞)→AM+1,Hh(H) is continuous. So, the mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {L} : C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )\rightarrow \mathcal {A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}(\mathbb {H}) $$\end{document}L:C{M}(0,∞)→A{M+1}(H) is well-defined and continuous, and it is moreover injective.

Proof

Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}φ∈C{M}(0,∞), and choose h>0h>0 such that (2.13) holds. Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \in \mathbb {H}$$\end{document}ζ∈H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}p∈N0, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Re (ix\zeta )=-x\Im (\zeta )< 0$$\end{document}ℜ(ixζ)=-xℑ(ζ)<0 for every x> 0x>0, we have2.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&|(\mathcal {L}(\varphi ))^{(p)}(\zeta )|\le \int _0^\infty x^p|\varphi (x)|\textrm{d}x \\&\quad \le \int _0^{hm_p}\! x^p |\varphi (x)|\textrm{d}x + s_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}^0(\varphi )\int _{hm_p}^{hm_{p+1}}\! x^p \frac{h^{p+1}M_{p+1}}{x^{p+1}}\textrm{d}x +\int _{hm_{p+1}}^\infty \!\! \frac{x^{p+2}|\varphi (x)|}{x^2}\textrm{d}x \nonumber \\&\quad \le s_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}^0(\varphi )\left( hm_ph^pM_p+ h^{p+1}M_{p+1}\log \left( \frac{m_{p+1}}{m_p}\right) +h^{p+2}M_{p+2}\frac{1}{hm_{p+1}}\right) \nonumber \\&\quad \le s_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}^0(\varphi )h^{p+1}M_{p+1}\left( 2+C_0H^{p+1}\right) \le (2+C_0H)hs_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}^0(\varphi )(Hh)^pM_{p+1},\nonumber \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|(L(φ))(p)(ζ)|≤∫0∞xp|φ(x)|dx≤∫0hmpxp|φ(x)|dx+sM,h0(φ)∫hmphmp+1xphp+1Mp+1xp+1dx+∫hmp+1∞xp+2|φ(x)|x2dx≤sM,h0(φ)hmphpMp+hp+1Mp+1logmp+1mp+hp+2Mp+21hmp+1≤sM,h0(φ)hp+1Mp+12+C0Hp+1≤(2+C0H)hsM,h0(φ)(Hh)pMp+1,where in the next-to-last inequality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) has been applied. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L is well-defined and continuous from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}CM,h(0,∞) into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1},hH}(\mathbb {H})$$\end{document}AM+1,hH(H).

The proof of injectivity can be found in [4, Lemma 2.10]. □ □

Remark 2.13

If we suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M satisfies the stronger condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc) instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm), then the Laplace transform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}C{M}(0,∞) into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(\mathbb {H})$$\end{document}A{M}(H). This is easily seen by splitting the integral in (2.14) into only two subintervals, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,hm_{p})$$\end{document}(0,hmp) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(hm_p,\infty )$$\end{document}(hmp,∞) and estimating similarly.

A Modified Stieltjes Moment Problem in Gelfand-Shilov Spaces

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence. The p-th moment, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}p∈N0, of an element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}φ∈C{M}(0,∞) is defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mu _p(\varphi ) := \int _0^\infty x^p \varphi (x) \textrm{d}x . $$\end{document}μp(φ):=∫0∞xpφ(x)dx.The formula3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}(\varphi )^{(p)}(0) = i^p \mu _p(\varphi ), \qquad \varphi \in C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty ), p \in \mathbb {N}_0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(φ)(p)(0)=ipμp(φ),φ∈C{M}(0,∞),p∈N0,guarantees, according to Lema 2.12, that whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) the Stieltjes moment mapping\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {M}: C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )\rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}};\ \varphi \mapsto (\mu _p(\varphi ))_p $$\end{document}M:C{M}(0,∞)→Λ{M+1};φ↦(μp(φ))pis well-defined and continuous. Indeed, for every h>0h>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}φ∈CM,h(0,∞) one has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc), Remark 2.13 shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}$$\end{document}M sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}C{M}(0,∞) into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}$$\end{document}Λ{M}. This latter situation was studied in [4], while the former one is our objective now. In order to stress the relevance of conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) for the Stieltjes moment problem to be well-posed with one or another target space, we need to introduce the concept of optimal flat functions in ultraholomorphic classes in unbounded sectors of the Riemann surface of the logarithm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}$$\end{document}R. We refer to [12] for further details.

Definition 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\subset \mathcal {R}$$\end{document}S⊂R be an unbounded sector bisected by direction d=0d=0, i.e., by the positive real line (0,+∞ )(0,+∞). A holomorphic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G:S\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}G:S→C is called an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$$\end{document}{M}-flat function in S if: (i)There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1,K_2>0$$\end{document}K1,K2>0 such that for all x>0x>0, 3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K_1h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_2x)\le G(x). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K1hM(K2x)≤G(x).(ii)There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_3,K_4>0$$\end{document}K3,K4>0 such that for all z∈ Sz∈S, one has 3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |G(z)|\le K_3h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_4|z|). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|G(z)|≤K3hM(K4|z|).

There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1,K_2>0$$\end{document}K1,K2>0 such that for all x>0x>0, 3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K_1h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_2x)\le G(x). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K1hM(K2x)≤G(x).

There exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_3,K_4>0$$\end{document}K3,K4>0 such that for all z∈ Sz∈S, one has 3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |G(z)|\le K_3h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}(K_4|z|). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|G(z)|≤K3hM(K4|z|).

We note that the estimate in (3.3) amounts to the fact that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|G(z)|\le K_3K_4^pM_p|z|^p,\qquad p\in \mathbb {N}_0,\ z\in S, $$\end{document}|G(z)|≤K3K4pMp|z|p,p∈N0,z∈S,what exactly says that G admits uniform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$$\end{document}{M}-asymptotic expansion in S given by the null power series, and this is the meaning of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$$\end{document}{M}-flatness. The inequality (3.2) makes the function optimal in a sense, as its rate of decrease on the positive real axis when t tends to 0 is accurately specified by the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$$\end{document}hM. The following result is interesting for our discussion.

Proposition 3.2

([12, Prop. 3.10]). The following result, partially obtained in [12, Prop. 3.11], shows the key role of such kernel functions. We include the whole proof for the reader’s convenience.

Proposition 3.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$$\end{document}γ(M)>0. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty ))\subset \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}$$\end{document}M(C{M}(0,∞))⊂Λ{M} if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc).

Proof

Similarly, we have the following characterization, again partially included in [13].

Proposition 3.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$$\end{document}γ(M)>0. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty ))\subset \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}M(C{M}(0,∞))⊂Λ{M+1} if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm).

Proof

As previously said, Lemma 2.12 implies that the condition is sufficient. Suppose now that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty ))\subset \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}M(C{M}(0,∞))⊂Λ{M+1}, and consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}e∈C{M}(0,∞) as before, so that there exist C,h>0C,h>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _p(e)\le Ch^pM_{p+1}$$\end{document}μp(e)≤ChpMp+1 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}p∈N0. On the other hand, by the left-hand inequalities in (3.4), for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}p∈N0 we have that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _p(e)&\ge \int _{K_2m_p}^{K_2m_{p+1}} t^p e(t)\,dt \ge K_1 \int _{K_2m_p}^{K_2m_{p+1}} t^p h_{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\left( \frac{K_2}{t}\right) \,dt\\&=K_1K_2^{p+1}M_{p+1}\log \left( \frac{m_{p+1}}{m_p}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μp(e)≥∫K2mpK2mp+1tpe(t)dt≥K1∫K2mpK2mp+1tphMK2tdt=K1K2p+1Mp+1logmp+1mp,where the last equality is a consequence of (2.4). Analogously as before, the estimates for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _p(e)$$\end{document}μp(e) from above and below imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) is satisfied. □ □

Remark 3.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>0$$\end{document}γ(M)>0. From the previous two results one easily concludes that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc) (respectively, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm)) if, and only if, for every kernel function e associated with an optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}$$\end{document}{M}-flat function G in some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_\gamma $$\end{document}Sγ one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu _p(e))_p\approx {\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}(μp(e))p≈M (resp. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu _p(e))_p\approx {\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}$$\end{document}(μp(e))p≈M+1).

We will reduce, via the Laplace transform, the study of the injectivity and surjectivity of the Stieltjes moment mapping in this new setting to their counterparts for the asymptotic Borel mapping (Theorems 2.7 and 2.6), as it was already done by A. L. Durán and R. Estrada in [5], and later on by several authors [1, 4, 15, 16].

The next lemma provides an auxiliary function, already appearing in the work [5] and later adapted to our needs, see [4]. We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{-1} = \{ z \in \mathbb {C}\, | \, \Im m \, z > -1 \}$$\end{document}H-1={z∈C|ℑmz>-1}.

Lemma 3.6

([4, Lemma 3.1]). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{A}}$$\end{document}A be a sequence satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(nq)}$$\end{document}(nq), and such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}$$\end{document}A^ is a weight sequence. Then, there is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0 \in \mathcal {O}(\mathbb {H}_{-1})$$\end{document}G0∈O(H-1) satisfying the following conditions: (i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 does not vanish on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{-1}$$\end{document}H-1.(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \sup _{z \in \mathbb {H}_{-1}} |G_0(z)|e^{\omega _{\widehat{A}}(|z|)} < \infty $$\end{document}supz∈H-1|G0(z)|eωA^(|z|)<∞.(iii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \sup _{p \in \mathbb {N}}\sup _{x \in \mathbb {R}} \frac{|G_0^{(p)}(x)|e^{\omega _{\widehat{A}}(|x|/2)}}{2^pp!} < \infty $$\end{document}supp∈Nsupx∈R|G0(p)(x)|eωA^(|x|/2)2pp!<∞.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 does not vanish on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{-1}$$\end{document}H-1.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \sup _{z \in \mathbb {H}_{-1}} |G_0(z)|e^{\omega _{\widehat{A}}(|z|)} < \infty $$\end{document}supz∈H-1|G0(z)|eωA^(|z|)<∞.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \sup _{p \in \mathbb {N}}\sup _{x \in \mathbb {R}} \frac{|G_0^{(p)}(x)|e^{\omega _{\widehat{A}}(|x|/2)}}{2^pp!} < \infty $$\end{document}supp∈Nsupx∈R|G0(p)(x)|eωA^(|x|/2)2pp!<∞.

Proposition 2.10 and Lemma 3.6 imply the following general result. A similar proof for classical Gelfand-Shilov spaces (i.e., when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M is a Gevrey sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(p!^\alpha )_p$$\end{document}(p!α)p with α >1α>1) can be found in [15, Prop. 4.13], and a similar statement for strongly regular sequences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>1$$\end{document}γ(M)>1 was included in [16, Prop. 6.6] without proof. Another version, disregarding continuity and under stronger conditions than the ones imposed here, appeared in [4, Lemma 3.3]. Note that, as indicated in Subsection 2.2, the conditions imposed on the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{A}}$$\end{document}A are precisely those guaranteeing that the class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}^{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\}}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}S{M}{A^}(0,∞) is non-trivial.

Lemma 3.7

We are ready to study the injectivity and surjectivity of the Stieltjes moment mapping.

Theorem 3.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(p!)_p\subset {\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}(p!)p⊂M, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{A}}$$\end{document}A be a sequence satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(nq)}$$\end{document}(nq) and such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}$$\end{document}A^ is a weight sequence. Then, the following statements are equivalent: (i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \sum _{p = 0}^\infty \frac{1}{m_p^{1/2}} = \infty $$\end{document}∑p=0∞1mp1/2=∞.(ii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}: \mathcal {A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}(\mathbb {H}) \rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}B:A{M+1}(H)→Λ{M+1} is injective.(iii)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}: C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )\rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}M:C{M}(0,∞)→Λ{M+1} is injective.(iv)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}: \mathcal {S}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty ) \rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}M:S{M}(0,∞)→Λ{M+1} is injective.(v)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}: \mathcal {S}^{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\}}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty ) \rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}M:S{M}{A^}(0,∞)→Λ{M+1} is injective.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \sum _{p = 0}^\infty \frac{1}{m_p^{1/2}} = \infty $$\end{document}∑p=0∞1mp1/2=∞.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}: \mathcal {A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}(\mathbb {H}) \rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}B:A{M+1}(H)→Λ{M+1} is injective.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}: C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )\rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}M:C{M}(0,∞)→Λ{M+1} is injective.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}: \mathcal {S}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty ) \rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}M:S{M}(0,∞)→Λ{M+1} is injective.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}: \mathcal {S}^{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\}}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty ) \rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}M:S{M}{A^}(0,∞)→Λ{M+1} is injective.

Proof

(i) ⇒ (ii)(i)⇒(ii): By Theorem 2.7.

(ii) ⇒ (iii)(ii)⇒(iii): Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}φ∈C{M}(0,∞) be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _p(\varphi ) = 0$$\end{document}μp(φ)=0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}p∈N0. By Lemma 2.12 we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(\varphi ) \in \mathcal {A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}(\mathbb {H})$$\end{document}L(φ)∈A{M+1}(H). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L is injective (Lemma 2.12), we obtain that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \equiv 0$$\end{document}φ≡0.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(iii) \Rightarrow (iv) \Rightarrow (v)$$\end{document}(iii)⇒(iv)⇒(v): Obvious, see Remark 2.11.

(v) ⇒ (i)(v)⇒(i): In view of Theorem 2.7 it suffices to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}: \mathcal {A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}(\mathbb {H}) \rightarrow \Lambda _{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}$$\end{document}B:A{M+1}(H)→Λ{M+1} is injective. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \mathcal {A}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\}}(\mathbb {H})$$\end{document}f∈A{M+1}(H) be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{(p)}(0) = 0$$\end{document}f(p)(0)=0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}p∈N0. Consider the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 from Lemma 3.6. By Lemma 3.7 we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(fG_0)|_{\mathbb {R}} = \widehat{\varphi }$$\end{document}(fG0)|R=φ^ for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in \mathcal {S}^{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\}}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}φ∈S{M}{A^}(0,∞). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_0$$\end{document}G0 does not vanish (Lemma 3.6(i)), we obtain that f ≡ 0f≡0. □ □

In the case of surjectivity, it will frequently come with local extension operators, right inverses for the Borel, respectively, the moment mapping, with a uniform scaling of the constant h determining the Banach spaces under consideration.

Theorem 3.9

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm), and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{A}}$$\end{document}A be a sequence satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(nq)}$$\end{document}(nq) and such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}$$\end{document}A^ is a weight sequence. Then:

In the proof of Theorem 3.9 we shall use the following lemma, inspired by [5].

Lemma 3.10

([4, Lemma 3.6]). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(c_p)_p \in \mathbb {C}^\mathbb {N}$$\end{document}(cp)p∈CN and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G \in C^\infty ((-\delta ,\delta ))$$\end{document}G∈C∞((-δ,δ)), for some δ > 0δ>0, such that G(0) ≠ 0G(0)≠0. Set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ b_p = \sum _{j = 0}^p \left( {\begin{array}{c}p\\ j\end{array}}\right) c_j \left( \frac{1}{G} \right) ^{(p-j)} (0), \qquad p \in \mathbb {N}_0. $$\end{document}bp=∑j=0ppjcj1G(p-j)(0),p∈N0.Then,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _{j=0}^p \left( {\begin{array}{c}p\\ j\end{array}}\right) b_j G^{(p-j)}(0) = c_p, \qquad p\in \mathbb {N}_0. $$\end{document}∑j=0ppjbjG(p-j)(0)=cp,p∈N0.

Proof of Theorem 3.9

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i') \Rightarrow (ii') \Rightarrow (iii')$$\end{document}(i′)⇒(ii′)⇒(iii′) Clear by the inclusions in Remark 2.11.

It is evident that any of the first four statements (*)(∗) implies the corresponding (*')(∗′), and so any of the statements from (i) to (iv) implies all the equivalent conditions from (i')(i′) to (iv')(iv′).

(v')⇒ (iv)(v′)⇒(iv) Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}$$\end{document}M+1 clearly share the same gamma index, (v')(v′) amounts to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1})>2$$\end{document}γ(M+1)>2. \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}\circ W_h$$\end{document}M∘Wh is the identity map in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{{\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1},h}$$\end{document}ΛM+1,h by arguing as in (iv')⇒ (i')(iv′)⇒(i′).

(III) Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc) for the weight sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M amounts to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\approx {\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M+1≈M, the substitution keeps the considered spaces unchanged. Then, it suffices to apply Theorem 2.6 to see that (iv')⇒ (v')(iv′)⇒(v′).

(IV) If both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{A}}$$\end{document}A satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\approx {\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M+1≈M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}})_{+1}\approx \widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}$$\end{document}(A^)+1≈A^, and so the statements (i) and (v) are equivalent. This fact and the previous implications allow for the conclusion. □ □

Remark 3.11

The equivalence of the five conditions (i')-(v')(i′)-(v′) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M is strongly regular (and so also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}\approx {\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M+1≈M) was already shown in [4, Thm. 3.5], while the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc) is deduced in [3, Thm. 6.1.(b) and Thm. 7.2.(b)]. The novelty in this situation consists in the equivalence with (iv). One should also note that in [3] the Stieltjes moment problem is also solved for Beurling-like classes, and the existence of global right inverses in both the Roumieu and Beurling classes is characterized. However, the techniques used there seem to heavily depend on the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc) (see, for example, Lemmas 3.6.(b) and 3.7.(b) and Proposition 5.1 in [3]), so they are not available under the weaker condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm). Nevertheless, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) allows for the construction of the local extension operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{h}$$\end{document}Vh in (iv), and this has been the motivation for this new insight. Note that, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{A}}$$\end{document}A are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc), the construction of local right inverses for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}$$\end{document}M as the ones in (i)-(iii)(i)-(iii), with a uniform scaling of the parameter h entering the definition of the corresponding Banach spaces, is new, although (ii) was previously obtained in [16] when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M is strongly regular.

Remark 3.12

As indicated at the end of Subsection 2.1 for the Borel mapping, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(dc)}$$\end{document}(dc) one can deduce that the moment mapping will never be bijective in any of the considered frameworks, since Theorem 3.8 would yield \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ({\boldsymbol{M}})\le 2$$\end{document}ω(M)≤2 while Theorem 3.9 would imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ({\boldsymbol{M}})>2$$\end{document}γ(M)>2, and both inequalities are not compatible. We will make a more general statement with a detailed proof in Corollary 4.7.

The Situation When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) Fails to Hold

\setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}_{+1}$$\end{document}M+1. So, the consideration of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$$\end{document}M~ makes sense precisely when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) fails. For example, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}=(q^{p^p})_p$$\end{document}M=(qp)p we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$$\end{document}M~ is equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M_{p+1}\cdot p^p)_p$$\end{document}(Mp+1·pp)p.

Remark 4.1

Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M is a sequence such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\inf _{p\in \mathbb {N}_0}m_p>0$$\end{document}infp∈N0mp>0. Then, as indicated in [13, Remark 2.4], the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) can be rephrased as4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \exists C_1> 0,\ H > 1:\log (m_p)\le C_1H^p,\quad p\in \mathbb {N}_0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∃C1>0,H>1:log(mp)≤C1Hp,p∈N0.In particular, this holds if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(lc)}$$\end{document}(lc); if for such an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M we had \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{p\rightarrow \infty }m_p<\infty $$\end{document}limp→∞mp<∞, then (4.2) would be clearly valid. In other words, an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(lc)}$$\end{document}(lc) sequence not satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(sm)}$$\end{document}(sm) has to be a weight sequence. In the sequel, we will always consider this situation.

The following results are straightforward adaptations of Propositions 2.8 and 2.10, respectively.

Proposition 4.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{M}}$$\end{document}M be a weight sequence such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$$\end{document}M~ is almost increasing, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{A}}$$\end{document}A be either an almost increasing sequence, or a sequence such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\liminf _{p\rightarrow \infty }A_p^{1/p}>0$$\end{document}lim infp→∞Ap1/p>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}$$\end{document}A^ satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {(alg)}$$\end{document}(alg). Then (i)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}:\mathcal {S}^{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\}}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(\mathbb {R})\rightarrow \mathcal {S}^{\{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\}}_{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\}}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}F:S{M}{A^}(R)→S{A^}{M~}(R) is continuous, and the same holds at the level of Banach spaces with a uniform scaling of the value h.(ii)The previous statement is valid also for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{-1}$$\end{document}F-1.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}:\mathcal {S}^{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\}}_{\{{\boldsymbol{M}}\}}(\mathbb {R})\rightarrow \mathcal {S}^{\{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{M}}}\}}_{\{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\}}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}F:S{M}{A^}(R)→S{A^}{M~}(R) is continuous, and the same holds at the level of Banach spaces with a uniform scaling of the value h.

The previous statement is valid also for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{-1}$$\end{document}F-1.

Proof

The proof of Proposition 2.8 initially works, except for the last line in (2.10), which cannot be written now. Gathering the valid estimates, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\sup _{\xi \in \mathbb {R}} |\xi ^q\widehat{\varphi }^{(p)}(\xi )|\\&\quad \le \sum _{j=0}^{\min \{p,q\}} \left( {\begin{array}{c}q\\ j\end{array}}\right) \left( {\begin{array}{c}p\\ j\end{array}}\right) j! 2s_{{\boldsymbol{M}},h}^{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{A}}},h}(\varphi )h^{p+q+1-2j}(q-j)!A_{q-j}M_{p+1-j}\log \left( \frac{e^2m_{p+1-j}}{m_{p-j}}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supξ∈R|ξqφ^(p)(ξ)|≤∑j=0min{p,q}qjpjj!2sM,hA^,h(φ)hp+q+1-2j(q-j)!Aq-jMp+1-jloge2mp+1-jmp-j.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{M}}}$$\end{document}M~ is assumed to be almost increasing, the rest of the proof of Proposition 2.8 can be easily adapted. □ □
